</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"/><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Christian_mysticism" title="Template:Christian mysticism"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Christian_mysticism" title="Template talk:Christian mysticism"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Christian_mysticism&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Teresa of Ávila</b>, OCD (born <b>Teresa Sánchez de Cepeda y Ahumada</b>; 28 March 1515&#160;&#8211;&#32;4&#160;or 15 October 1582),<sup id="cite_ref-julianGregorian_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-julianGregorian-4">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup>  also called <b>Saint Teresa of Jesus</b>, was a Spanish <a href="/wiki/Carmelite_nun" class="mw-redirect" title="Carmelite nun">Carmelite nun</a> and prominent <a href="/wiki/Spanish_mystic" class="mw-redirect" title="Spanish mystic">Spanish mystic</a> and <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Reformation" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholic Reformation">religious reformer</a>.
</p><p>Active during the <a href="/wiki/Counter-Reformation" title="Counter-Reformation">Counter-Reformation</a>, Teresa became the central figure of a movement of spiritual and monastic renewal, reforming the <a href="/wiki/Carmelite_Order" class="mw-redirect" title="Carmelite Order">Carmelite Orders</a> of both women and men.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELehfeldt2017217_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELehfeldt2017217-5">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> The movement was later joined by the younger Spanish Carmelite friar and mystic <a href="/wiki/John_of_the_Cross" title="John of the Cross">John of the Cross</a>, with whom she established the <a href="/wiki/Discalced_Carmelites" title="Discalced Carmelites">Discalced Carmelites</a>. A formal papal decree adopting the split from the old order was issued in 1580.<sup id="cite_ref-encyclopedia.com_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-encyclopedia.com-6">&#91;web 3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Her autobiography, <i>The Life of Teresa of Jesus</i>, <i><a href="/wiki/The_Interior_Castle" class="mw-redirect" title="The Interior Castle">The Interior Castle</a></i>, and <i>The <a href="/wiki/Way_of_Perfection" class="mw-redirect" title="Way of Perfection">Way of Perfection</a></i>, are prominent works on <a href="/wiki/Christian_mysticism" title="Christian mysticism">Christian mysticism</a> and <a href="/wiki/Christian_meditation" title="Christian meditation">Christian meditation</a> practice. In her autobiography, written as a defense of her ecstatic mystical experiences, she discerns four stages in the ascent of the <a href="/wiki/Soul_(spirit)" class="mw-redirect" title="Soul (spirit)">soul</a> to God: <a href="/wiki/Mental_prayer" title="Mental prayer">mental prayer</a> and meditation; the prayer of quiet; absorption-in-God; ecstatic consciousness.
</p><p>Forty years after her death, in 1622, Teresa was <a href="/wiki/Canonized" class="mw-redirect" title="Canonized">canonized</a> by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_XV" title="Pope Gregory XV">Pope Gregory XV</a>. On 27 September 1970 <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_VI" title="Pope Paul VI">Pope Paul VI</a> proclaimed Teresa the first female <i><a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctor of the Church</a></i> in recognition of her centuries-long spiritual legacy to <a href="/wiki/Catholicism" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholicism">Catholicism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPope_Paul_VI1970a_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPope_Paul_VI1970a-7">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-catholicnewsagency.com_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-catholicnewsagency.com-8">&#91;web 4&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Biography">Biography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Biography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Teresa Sánchez de Cepeda y Ahumada was born in 1515 in <a href="/wiki/%C3%81vila,_Spain" class="mw-redirect" title="Ávila, Spain">Ávila, Spain</a>. Her paternal grandfather, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Juan_S%C3%A1nchez_de_Toledo&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Juan Sánchez de Toledo (page does not exist)">Juan Sánchez de Toledo</a>, was a <span title="Spanish-language text"><i lang="es"><a href="/wiki/Marrano" title="Marrano">marrano</a></i></span> or <span title="Spanish-language text"><i lang="es"><a href="/wiki/Converso" title="Converso">converso</a></i></span>, a Jew forced to convert to Christianity or emigrate. When Teresa's father was a child, Juan was condemned by the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Inquisition" title="Spanish Inquisition">Spanish Inquisition</a> for allegedly returning to <a href="/wiki/Judaism" title="Judaism">Judaism</a>, but he was later able to assume a Catholic identity.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFoa2015_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFoa2015-9">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Her father, <a href="https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alonso_S%C3%A1nchez_de_Cepeda" class="extiw" title="es:Alonso Sánchez de Cepeda">Alonso Sánchez de Cepeda</a>, was a successful wool merchant and one of the wealthiest men in Ávila. He bought a <a href="/wiki/Knighthood" class="mw-redirect" title="Knighthood">knighthood</a> and assimilated successfully into Christian society.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Arnold_van_Westerhout_-_Teresa_of_%C3%81vila_leaves_her_home_to_travel_to_Africa.jpeg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Arnold_van_Westerhout_-_Teresa_of_%C3%81vila_leaves_her_home_to_travel_to_Africa.jpeg/220px-Arnold_van_Westerhout_-_Teresa_of_%C3%81vila_leaves_her_home_to_travel_to_Africa.jpeg" decoding="async" width="220" height="307" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Arnold_van_Westerhout_-_Teresa_of_%C3%81vila_leaves_her_home_to_travel_to_Africa.jpeg/330px-Arnold_van_Westerhout_-_Teresa_of_%C3%81vila_leaves_her_home_to_travel_to_Africa.jpeg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Arnold_van_Westerhout_-_Teresa_of_%C3%81vila_leaves_her_home_to_travel_to_Africa.jpeg/440px-Arnold_van_Westerhout_-_Teresa_of_%C3%81vila_leaves_her_home_to_travel_to_Africa.jpeg 2x" data-file-width="524" data-file-height="732" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Arnold_van_Westerhout_-_Teresa_of_%C3%81vila_leaves_her_home_to_travel_to_Africa.jpeg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><i>Teresa of Ávila elopes to travel to Africa</i> by <a href="/wiki/Arnold_van_Westerhout" title="Arnold van Westerhout">Arnold van Westerhout</a></div></div></div>
<p>Previously married to <a href="/w/index.php?title=Catalina_del_Peso_y_Henao&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Catalina del Peso y Henao (page does not exist)">Catalina del Peso y Henao</a>, with whom he had three children, in 1509, Sánchez de Cepeda married Teresa's mother, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Beatriz_de_Ahumada_y_Cuevas&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Beatriz de Ahumada y Cuevas (page does not exist)">Beatriz de Ahumada y Cuevas</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Gotarrendura" title="Gotarrendura">Gotarrendura</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEClissold1982_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClissold1982-10">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Teresa's mother brought her up as a dedicated Christian. Fascinated by accounts of the lives of the saints, she ran away from home at age seven, with her brother Rodrigo, to seek <a href="/wiki/Martyrdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Martyrdom">martyrdom</a> in the fight against <a href="/wiki/The_Moors" class="mw-redirect" title="The Moors">the Moors</a>. Her uncle brought them home, when he spotted them just outside the town walls.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMedwick1999Expeditions_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMedwick1999Expeditions-11">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When Teresa was eleven years old, her mother died, leaving her grief-stricken. This prompted her to embrace a deeper devotion to the <a href="/wiki/Virgin_Mary" class="mw-redirect" title="Virgin Mary">Virgin Mary</a> as her spiritual mother. Teresa was also enamored of popular fiction, which at the time consisted primarily of medieval tales of <a href="/wiki/Knighthood" class="mw-redirect" title="Knighthood">knighthood</a> and works about fashion, gardens and flowers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETeresa_of_AvilaLewis1870_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETeresa_of_AvilaLewis1870-12">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-CNA_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CNA-13">&#91;web 5&#93;</a></sup> Teresa was sent to the <a href="/wiki/Augustinians" title="Augustinians">Augustinian</a> nuns' school at <a href="/wiki/%C3%81vila,_Spain" class="mw-redirect" title="Ávila, Spain">Ávila</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZimmerman1912_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZimmerman1912-14">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religious_life">Religious life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Religious life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Ascetic_and_mystical_practice">Ascetic and mystical practice</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Ascetic and mystical practice">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>After completing her education, she initially resisted the idea of a religious vocation, but after a stay with her uncle and other relatives, she relented. In 1534, aged 20,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPirlo1997241_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPirlo1997241-15">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> much to the disappointment of her pious and austere father, she decided to enter the local easy-going Carmelite <i>Convent of the Incarnation</i>, significantly built on top of land that had been used previously as a burial ground for Jews. She took up religious reading on contemplative prayer, especially <a href="/wiki/Francisco_de_Osuna" title="Francisco de Osuna">Osuna</a>'s <i>Abecedario espiritual</i> ("Third Spiritual Alphabet," 1527), a guide on <a href="/wiki/Examination_of_conscience" title="Examination of conscience">examination of conscience</a> and "spiritual self-concentration and inner contemplation, known in mystical nomenclatura as <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">oratio recollectionis</i></span>."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHerzogSchaffHauck1908412_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHerzogSchaffHauck1908412-16">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> She also dipped into other mystical <a href="/wiki/Ascetic" class="mw-redirect" title="Ascetic">ascetical</a> works such as the <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">Tractatus de oratione et meditatione</i></span> of <a href="/wiki/Peter_of_Alcantara" class="mw-redirect" title="Peter of Alcantara">Peter of Alcantara</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHerzogSchaffHauck1908412_16-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHerzogSchaffHauck1908412-16">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Her zeal for mortification caused her to become ill again and she spent almost a year in bed, causing huge worry to her community and family. She nearly died but she recovered, attributing her recovery to the miraculous intercession of Saint Joseph. She began to experience bouts of <a href="/wiki/Religious_ecstasy" title="Religious ecstasy">religious ecstasy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEClissold1982_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClissold1982-10">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> She reported that, during her illness, she had progressed from the lowest stage of "recollection", to the "devotions of silence" and even to the "devotions of ecstasy", which was one of perceived "perfect union with God" (see <a href="#Mysticism">§&#160;Mysticism</a>). During this final stage, she said she frequently experienced the rich "blessing of tears". As the Catholic distinction between <a href="/wiki/Mortal_sin" title="Mortal sin">mortal</a> and <a href="/wiki/Venial_sin" title="Venial sin">venial sin</a> became clear to her, she came to understand the awful horror of sin and the inherent nature of <a href="/wiki/Original_sin" title="Original sin">original sin</a>. She also became conscious of her own natural impotence in confronting sin and the need for absolute surrender to <a href="/wiki/God" title="God">God</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Around the same time, she received a copy of the full Spanish translation of <a href="/wiki/Augustine_of_Hippo" title="Augustine of Hippo">Augustine of Hippo</a>'s autobiographical work <i><a href="/wiki/Confessions_(Augustine)" title="Confessions (Augustine)">Confessions</a></i>, which helped her resolve and to tend to her own bouts of religious <a href="/wiki/Conscience" title="Conscience">scruples</a>. The text helped her realize that holiness was indeed possible and she found solace in the idea that such a great saint was once an inveterate sinner. In her autobiography, she wrote that she "was very fond of St. Augustine<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>[...] for he was a sinner too."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETeresa_of_AvilaZimmerman1997_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETeresa_of_AvilaZimmerman1997-17">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Transverberation"><i>Transverberation</i></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Transverberation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Around 1556, friends suggested that her newfound knowledge could be of diabolical and not of divine origin. She had begun to inflict <a href="/wiki/Mortification_of_the_flesh" title="Mortification of the flesh">mortifications of the flesh</a> upon herself. But her <a href="/wiki/Confession_(sacrament)" class="mw-redirect" title="Confession (sacrament)">confessor</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Jesuit" class="mw-redirect" title="Jesuit">Jesuit</a> <a href="/wiki/Francis_Borgia" title="Francis Borgia">Francis Borgia</a>, reassured her of the divine inspiration of her thoughts. On <a href="/wiki/St._Peter%27s_Day" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Peter&#39;s Day">St. Peter's Day</a> in 1559, Teresa became firmly convinced that Jesus Christ had presented himself to her in bodily form, though invisible. These visions lasted almost uninterruptedly for more than two years. In another vision, the famous <i>transverberation</i>, a <a href="/wiki/Seraph" title="Seraph">seraph</a> drove the fiery point of a golden lance repeatedly through her heart, causing her an ineffable spiritual and bodily pain:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>I saw in his hand a long spear of gold, and at the point there seemed to be a little fire. He appeared to me to be thrusting it at times into my heart, and to pierce my very entrails; when he drew it out, he seemed to draw them out also, and to leave me all on fire with a great love of God. The pain was so great, that it made me moan; and yet so surpassing was the sweetness of this excessive pain, that I could not wish to be rid of it&#160;...<sup id="cite_ref-cherub_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cherub-18">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>The account of this vision was the inspiration for one of <a href="/wiki/Bernini" class="mw-redirect" title="Bernini">Bernini</a>'s most famous works, the <i><a href="/wiki/Ecstasy_of_Saint_Teresa" title="Ecstasy of Saint Teresa">Ecstasy of Saint Teresa</a></i> at <a href="/wiki/Santa_Maria_della_Vittoria,_Rome" title="Santa Maria della Vittoria, Rome">Santa Maria della Vittoria</a> in Rome. Although based in part on Teresa's description of her mystical <a href="/wiki/Transverberation_of_Saint_Teresa" class="mw-redirect" title="Transverberation of Saint Teresa">transverberation</a> in her autobiography, Bernini's depiction of the event is considered by some to be highly eroticized, especially when compared to the entire preceding artistic Teresian tradition.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The memory of this episode served as an inspiration throughout the rest of her life, and motivated her lifelong imitation of the life and suffering of Jesus, epitomized in the adage often associated with her: "Lord, either let me suffer or let me die."<sup id="cite_ref-:0_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-20">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Teresa, who became a celebrity in her town dispensing wisdom from behind the convent grille, was known for her raptures, which sometimes involved <a href="/wiki/Levitation" title="Levitation">levitation</a>. It was a source of embarrassment to her and she bade her sisters hold her down when this occurred. Subsequently, historians, neurologists and psychiatrists like <a href="/wiki/Peter_Fenwick_(neuropsychologist)" title="Peter Fenwick (neuropsychologist)">Peter Fenwick</a> and Javier Álvarez-Rodríguez, among others, have taken an interest in her symptomatology. The fact that she wrote down virtually everything that happened to her during her religious life means that an invaluable and exceedingly rare medical record from the 16th century has been preserved. Examination of this record has led to the speculative conclusion that she may have suffered from <a href="/wiki/Temporal_lobe_epilepsy" title="Temporal lobe epilepsy">temporal lobe epilepsy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarton1982_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarton1982-21">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERodriguez2007_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERodriguez2007-22">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Monastic_reformer">Monastic reformer</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Monastic reformer">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Over time, Teresa found herself increasingly at odds with the spiritual malaise prevailing in her convent of the Incarnation. Among the 150 nuns living there, the observance of <a href="/wiki/Cloister" title="Cloister">cloister</a>, designed to protect and strengthen spiritual practice and prayer, became so lax that it appeared to lose its purpose. The daily invasion of visitors, many of high social and political rank, disturbed the atmosphere with frivolous concerns and vacuous conversation. Such intrusions in the solitude essential to develop and sustain contemplative prayer so grieved Teresa that she longed to intervene.<sup id="cite_ref-ocd.pcn.net_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ocd.pcn.net-23">&#91;web 6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The incentive to take the practical steps inspired by her inward motivation was supported by the <a href="/wiki/Franciscan" class="mw-redirect" title="Franciscan">Franciscan</a> priest, <a href="/wiki/Peter_of_Alcantara" class="mw-redirect" title="Peter of Alcantara">Peter of Alcantara</a>, who met her early in 1560 and became her <a href="/wiki/Spiritual_direction" title="Spiritual direction">spiritual adviser</a>. She resolved to found a "reformed" Carmelite convent, correcting the laxity which she had found at the Incarnation convent and elsewhere besides. Guimara de Ulloa, a woman of wealth and a friend, supplied the funds for the project.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The abject poverty of the new convent, established in 1562 and named <a href="/wiki/Convento_de_San_Jos%C3%A9_(%C3%81vila)" title="Convento de San José (Ávila)">St. Joseph's (San José)</a>, at first caused a scandal among the citizens and authorities of Ávila, and the small house with its chapel was in peril of suppression. However, powerful patrons, including the local bishop, coupled with the impression of well ordered subsistence and purpose, turned animosity into approval.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In March 1563, after Teresa had moved to the new convent house, she received <a href="/wiki/Papal_decree" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal decree">papal sanction</a> for her primary principles of absolute poverty and renunciation of ownership of property, which she proceeded to formulate into a "constitution". Her plan was the revival of the earlier, stricter monastic rules, supplemented by new regulations including the three disciplines of ceremonial <a href="/wiki/Flagellation" title="Flagellation">flagellation</a> prescribed for the <a href="/wiki/Liturgy_of_the_Hours" title="Liturgy of the Hours">Divine Office</a> every week, and the <a href="/wiki/Discalceation" class="mw-redirect" title="Discalceation">discalceation</a> of the religious. For the first five years, Teresa remained in seclusion, mostly engaged in prayer and writing.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:TeresaAvila.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/TeresaAvila.jpg/220px-TeresaAvila.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="211" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/TeresaAvila.jpg/330px-TeresaAvila.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/TeresaAvila.jpg/440px-TeresaAvila.jpg 2x" data-file-width="521" data-file-height="500" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:TeresaAvila.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Church window at the <a href="/wiki/Iglesia-convento_de_Santa_Teresa" title="Iglesia-convento de Santa Teresa">Convent of St Teresa</a></div></div></div>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Extended_travels">Extended travels</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Extended travels">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In 1567, Teresa received a <a href="/wiki/Letters_patent" title="Letters patent">patent</a> from the Carmelite General, Rubeo de Ravenna, to establish further houses of the new <a href="/wiki/Religious_order_(Catholic)" title="Religious order (Catholic)">order</a>. This process required many visitations and long journeys across nearly all the provinces of Spain. She left a record of the arduous project in her <i>Libro de las Fundaciones</i>. Between 1567 and 1571, reformed convents were established at <a href="/wiki/Medina_del_Campo" title="Medina del Campo">Medina del Campo</a>, <a href="/wiki/Malag%C3%B3n" title="Malagón">Malagón</a>, <a href="/wiki/Valladolid" title="Valladolid">Valladolid</a>, <a href="/wiki/Toledo,_Spain" title="Toledo, Spain">Toledo</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pastrana,_Spain" title="Pastrana, Spain">Pastrana</a>, <a href="/wiki/Salamanca" title="Salamanca">Salamanca</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Alba_de_Tormes" title="Alba de Tormes">Alba de Tormes</a>.
</p><p>As part of the original patent, Teresa was given permission to set up two houses for <a href="/wiki/Friar" title="Friar">men</a> who wished to adopt the reforms. She convinced two Carmelite friars, <a href="/wiki/John_of_the_Cross" title="John of the Cross">John of the Cross</a> and Anthony of Jesus to help with this. They founded the first monastery of Discalced Carmelite brothers in November 1568 at <a href="/wiki/Duruelo" title="Duruelo">Duruelo</a>. Another friend of Teresa, <a href="/wiki/Jer%C3%B3nimo_Graci%C3%A1n" class="mw-redirect" title="Jerónimo Gracián">Jerónimo Gracián</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Carmelite_visitator" class="mw-redirect" title="Carmelite visitator">Carmelite visitator</a> of the older observance of <a href="/wiki/Andalusia" title="Andalusia">Andalusia</a> and apostolic commissioner, and later <a href="/wiki/Provincial_superior" title="Provincial superior">provincial</a> of the Teresian order, gave her powerful support in founding monasteries at <a href="/wiki/Segovia" title="Segovia">Segovia</a> (1571), <a href="/wiki/Beas_de_Segura" title="Beas de Segura">Beas de Segura</a> (1574), <a href="/wiki/Seville" title="Seville">Seville</a> (1575), and <a href="/wiki/Caravaca_de_la_Cruz" title="Caravaca de la Cruz">Caravaca de la Cruz</a> (<a href="/wiki/Murcia" title="Murcia">Murcia</a>, 1576). Meanwhile, John of the Cross promoted the inner life of the movement through his power as a teacher and preacher.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKavanaughRodgriguez19919–27_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKavanaughRodgriguez19919–27-24">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Opposition_to_reforms">Opposition to reforms</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Opposition to reforms">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In 1576, unreformed members of the Carmelite order began to persecute Teresa, her supporters and her reforms. Following a number of resolutions adopted at the <a href="/wiki/General_chapter" class="mw-redirect" title="General chapter">general chapter</a> at <a href="/wiki/Piacenza" title="Piacenza">Piacenza</a>, the governing body of the order forbade all further founding of reformed convents. The general chapter instructed her to go into "voluntary" retirement at one of her institutions.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKavanaughRodgriguez19919–27_24-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKavanaughRodgriguez19919–27-24">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> She obeyed and chose St. Joseph's at Toledo. Meanwhile, her friends and associates were subjected to further attacks.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKavanaughRodgriguez19919–27_24-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKavanaughRodgriguez19919–27-24">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Several years later, her appeals by letter to King <a href="/wiki/Philip_II_of_Spain" title="Philip II of Spain">Philip II of Spain</a> secured relief. As a result, in 1579, the cases before the <a href="/wiki/Inquisition" title="Inquisition">inquisition</a> against her, Gracián and others, were dropped.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKavanaughRodgriguez19919–27_24-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKavanaughRodgriguez19919–27-24">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> This allowed the reform to resume. An edict from <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_XIII" title="Pope Gregory XIII">Pope Gregory XIII</a> allowed the appointment of a special provincial for the newer branch of the Carmelite religious, and a royal decree created a "protective" board of four assessors for the reform.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKavanaughRodgriguez19919–27_24-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKavanaughRodgriguez19919–27-24">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the last three years of her life, Teresa founded convents at <a href="/wiki/Villanueva_de_la_Jara" title="Villanueva de la Jara">Villanueva de la Jara</a> in northern <a href="/wiki/Andalusia" title="Andalusia">Andalusia</a> (1580), <a href="/wiki/Palencia" title="Palencia">Palencia</a> (1580), <a href="/wiki/Soria" title="Soria">Soria</a> (1581), <a href="/wiki/Burgos" title="Burgos">Burgos</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Granada" title="Granada">Granada</a> (1582). In total, seventeen convents, all but one founded by her, and as many men's monasteries, were owed to her reforms over twenty years.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESalamony2017_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESalamony2017-25">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Last_days">Last days</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Last days">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3><p>
Her final illness overtook her on one of her journeys from Burgos to Alba de Tormes. She died in 1582, just as <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Europe" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholic Europe">Catholic Europe</a> was making the switch from the <a href="/wiki/Julian_calendar" title="Julian calendar">Julian</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Gregorian_calendar" title="Gregorian calendar">Gregorian calendar</a>, which required the excision of the dates of 5–14 October from the calendar. She died either before midnight of 4 October or early in the morning of 15 October, which is celebrated as her feast day. According to the liturgical calendar then in use, she died on the 15th. Her last words were: "My Lord, it is time to move on. Well then, may your will be done. O my Lord and my Spouse, the hour that I have longed for has come. It is time to meet one another."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECounsell2011207_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECounsell2011207-26">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup></p><div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:281px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Avila_SaintTheresa1.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Avila_SaintTheresa1.jpg/279px-Avila_SaintTheresa1.jpg" decoding="async" width="279" height="209" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Avila_SaintTheresa1.jpg/419px-Avila_SaintTheresa1.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Avila_SaintTheresa1.jpg/558px-Avila_SaintTheresa1.jpg 2x" data-file-width="926" data-file-height="695" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Avila_SaintTheresa1.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/%C3%81vila,_Spain" class="mw-redirect" title="Ávila, Spain">Avila</a>, Saint Theresa's statue</div></div></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="After_her_death">After her death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: After her death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Holy_relics">Holy relics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Holy relics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1097763485">.mw-parser-output .ambox{border:1px solid #a2a9b1;border-left:10px solid #36c;background-color:#fbfbfb;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output .ambox-delete{border-left:10px solid #b32424}.mw-parser-output .ambox-content{border-left:10px solid #f28500}.mw-parser-output .ambox-style{border-left:10px solid #fc3}.mw-parser-output .ambox-move{border-left:10px solid #9932cc}.mw-parser-output .ambox-protection{border-left:10px solid #a2a9b1}.mw-parser-output .ambox .mbox-text{border:none;padding:0.25em .mbox-text-span{margin-left:23px!important}@media(min-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .ambox{margin:0 10%}}</style><table class="box-More_citations_needed_section plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-Refimprove" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><a href="/wiki/File:Question_book-new.svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/50px-Question_book-new.svg.png" decoding="async" width="50" height="39" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/75px-Question_book-new.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/100px-Question_book-new.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="399" /></a></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section <b>needs additional citations for <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">verification</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please help <a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit">improve this article</a> by <a href="/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners" title="Help:Referencing for beginners">adding citations to reliable sources</a>. Unsourced material may be challenged and removed.</span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">October 2020</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this template message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>She was buried at the Convento de la Anunciación in <a href="/wiki/Alba_de_Tormes" title="Alba de Tormes">Alba de Tormes</a>. Nine months after her death the coffin was opened and her body was found to be <a href="/wiki/Incorruptibility" title="Incorruptibility">intact</a> but the clothing had rotted. Before the body was re-interred one of her hands was cut off, wrapped in a scarf and sent to Ávila. Gracián cut the little finger off the hand and – according to his own account – kept it with him until it was taken by the occupying <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Turk" class="mw-redirect" title="Ottoman Turk">Ottoman Turks</a>, from whom he had to redeem it with a few rings and 20 reales. The body was exhumed again on 25 November 1585 to be moved to Ávila and found to be incorrupt. An arm was removed and left in Alba de Tormes at the nuns' request, to compensate for losing the main relic of Teresa, but the rest of the body was reburied in the Discalced Carmelite chapter house in Ávila. The removal was done without the approval of the Duke of Alba de Tormes and he brought the body back in 1586, with <a href="/wiki/Pope_Sixtus_V" title="Pope Sixtus V">Pope Sixtus V</a> ordering that it remain in Alba de Tormes on pain of excommunication. A grander tomb on the original site was raised in 1598 and the body was moved to a new chapel in 1616.
</p><p>The body still remains there, except for the following parts:
</p>
<ul><li>Rome – right foot and part of the upper jaw</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Lisbon" title="Lisbon">Lisbon</a> – hand</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Ronda" title="Ronda">Ronda</a>, Spain – left eye and left hand (the latter was kept by <a href="/wiki/Francisco_Franco" title="Francisco Franco">Francisco Franco</a> until his death after Francoist troops captured it from Republican troops during the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Civil_War" title="Spanish Civil War">Spanish Civil War</a>)</li>
<li>Museum of the Church of the Annunciation, Alba de Tormes – left arm and heart</li>
<li>Church of Our Lady of Loreto, <a href="/wiki/Paris" title="Paris">Paris</a>, France – one finger</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Sanl%C3%BAcar_de_Barrameda" title="Sanlúcar de Barrameda">Sanlúcar de Barrameda</a> – one finger</li></ul>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:225px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Ecstasy_of_Saint_Teresa_September_2015-2a.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/Ecstasy_of_Saint_Teresa_September_2015-2a.jpg/223px-Ecstasy_of_Saint_Teresa_September_2015-2a.jpg" decoding="async" width="223" height="353" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/Ecstasy_of_Saint_Teresa_September_2015-2a.jpg/334px-Ecstasy_of_Saint_Teresa_September_2015-2a.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/Ecstasy_of_Saint_Teresa_September_2015-2a.jpg/445px-Ecstasy_of_Saint_Teresa_September_2015-2a.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4242" data-file-height="6722" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Ecstasy_of_Saint_Teresa_September_2015-2a.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The <i><a href="/wiki/Ecstasy_of_Saint_Teresa" title="Ecstasy of Saint Teresa">Ecstasy of Saint Teresa</a></i> by <a href="/wiki/Bernini" class="mw-redirect" title="Bernini">Bernini</a>, Basilica of <a href="/wiki/Santa_Maria_della_Vittoria,_Rome" title="Santa Maria della Vittoria, Rome">Santa Maria della Vittoria, Rome</a></div></div></div>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Canonization">Canonization</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Canonization">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Palau_de_Mafra_-_Est%C3%A0tua_-_1.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3d/Palau_de_Mafra_-_Est%C3%A0tua_-_1.jpg/220px-Palau_de_Mafra_-_Est%C3%A0tua_-_1.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3d/Palau_de_Mafra_-_Est%C3%A0tua_-_1.jpg/330px-Palau_de_Mafra_-_Est%C3%A0tua_-_1.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3d/Palau_de_Mafra_-_Est%C3%A0tua_-_1.jpg/440px-Palau_de_Mafra_-_Est%C3%A0tua_-_1.jpg 2x" data-file-width="960" data-file-height="1280" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Palau_de_Mafra_-_Est%C3%A0tua_-_1.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Statue of Saint Teresa of Ávila in <a href="/wiki/Mafra_National_Palace" class="mw-redirect" title="Mafra National Palace">Mafra National Palace</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mafra,_Portugal" title="Mafra, Portugal">Mafra</a></div></div></div>
<p>In 1622, forty years after her death, she was <a href="/wiki/Canonized" class="mw-redirect" title="Canonized">canonized</a> by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_XV" title="Pope Gregory XV">Pope Gregory XV</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Cortes_Generales" title="Cortes Generales">Cortes</a> exalted her to <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patroness</a> of Spain in 1627. The <a href="/wiki/University_of_Salamanca" title="University of Salamanca">University of Salamanca</a> had granted her the title <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">Doctor ecclesiae</i></span> (<a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a> for "Doctor of the Church") with a diploma in her lifetime,<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Accuracy_dispute#Disputed_statement" title="Wikipedia:Accuracy dispute"><span title="The material near this tag is possibly inaccurate or nonfactual. (April 2021)">dubious</span></a>&#32;<span class="metadata"> &#8211; <a href="/wiki/Talk:Teresa_of_%C3%81vila#Salamanca_diploma" title="Talk:Teresa of Ávila">discuss</a></span></i>&#93;</sup> but that title is distinct from the papal honour of <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctor of the Church</a>, which is always conferred posthumously. The latter was finally bestowed upon her by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_VI" title="Pope Paul VI">Pope Paul VI</a> on 27 September 1970,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPope_Paul_VI1970a_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPope_Paul_VI1970a-7">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> along with <a href="/wiki/Catherine_of_Siena" title="Catherine of Siena">Catherine of Siena</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPope_Paul_VI1970b_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPope_Paul_VI1970b-27">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> making them the first women to be awarded the distinction. Teresa is revered as the Doctor of Prayer. The mysticism in her works exerted a formative influence upon many theologians of the following centuries, such as <a href="/wiki/Francis_of_Sales" class="mw-redirect" title="Francis of Sales">Francis of Sales</a>, <a href="/wiki/F%C3%A9nelon" class="mw-redirect" title="Fénelon">Fénelon</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Port-Royal-des-Champs" title="Port-Royal-des-Champs">Port-Royalists</a>. In 1670, her coffin was plated in silver.
</p><p>Teresa of Avila is honored in the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">Church of England</a> and in the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Episcopal_Church)" title="Calendar of saints (Episcopal Church)">Episcopal Church</a> on <a href="/wiki/October_15" title="October 15">15 October</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;web 7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-lff2018_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lff2018-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Patron_saint">Patron saint</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Patron saint">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In 1626, at the request of <a href="/wiki/Philip_IV_of_Spain" title="Philip IV of Spain">Philip IV of Spain</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Cortes_of_Castile_and_Le%C3%B3n" title="Cortes of Castile and León">Castilian parliament</a><sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup> elected Teresa "without lacking one vote" as copatron saint of Castile.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERowe201177_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERowe201177-30">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> This status was affirmed by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Urban_VIII" title="Pope Urban VIII">Pope Urban VIII</a> in a brief issued on 21 July 1627 in which he stated:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>For these reasons [the king's and Cortes's elections] and for the great devotion which they have for Teresa, they elected her for patron and advocate of these kingdoms in the last Cortes of the aforementioned kingdoms&#160;.... And because&#160;... the representatives in the Cortes desired it so greatly that their vote be firm and perpetual, we grant it our patronage and the approval of the <a href="/wiki/Holy_See" title="Holy See">Holy Apostolic See</a>.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8201;<cite><a href="#CITEREFRowe2011">Rowe 2011</a>, pp.&#160;77–78</cite></div></blockquote>
<p>More broadly, the 1620s, the entirety of Spain (Castile and beyond) debated who should be the country's <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saint</a>; the choices were either the current patron, <a href="/wiki/Saint_James_Matamoros" title="Saint James Matamoros">James Matamoros</a>, or a pairing of him and the newly canonised Saint Teresa of Ávila. Teresa's promoters said Spain faced newer challenges, especially the threat of Protestantism and societal decline at home, thus needing a more contemporary patron who understood those issues and could guide the Spanish nation. Santiago's supporters (<i>Santiaguistas</i>) fought back and eventually won the argument, but Teresa of Ávila remained far more popular at the local level.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERowe2011_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERowe2011-31">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (October 2020)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> <a href="/wiki/James_the_Great" title="James the Great">James the Great</a> kept the title of patron saint for the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Spanish people">Spanish people</a>, and the most <a href="/wiki/Blessed_Virgin_Mary" class="mw-redirect" title="Blessed Virgin Mary">Blessed Virgin Mary</a> under the title <a href="/wiki/Immaculate_Conception" title="Immaculate Conception">Immaculate Conception</a> as the sole patroness for the entire <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Spanish Kingdom">Spanish Kingdom</a>.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy_regarding_the_Infant_Jesus_of_Prague">Legacy regarding the Infant Jesus of Prague</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Legacy regarding the Infant Jesus of Prague">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:SantaTeresa.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/SantaTeresa.jpg/170px-SantaTeresa.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="213" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/SantaTeresa.jpg/255px-SantaTeresa.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/SantaTeresa.jpg/340px-SantaTeresa.jpg 2x" data-file-width="838" data-file-height="1050" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:SantaTeresa.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>"It is love alone that gives worth to all things."</div></div></div>
<p>The Spanish nuns who established <i>Carmel</i> in France brought a devotion to the <a href="/wiki/Infant_Jesus" class="mw-redirect" title="Infant Jesus">Infant Jesus</a> with them, and it became widespread in France.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDarricaun.d._32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDarricaun.d.-32">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-thereseoflisieux.org_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-thereseoflisieux.org-33">&#91;web 8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Though there are no written historical accounts establishing that Teresa of Ávila ever owned the famous <a href="/wiki/Infant_Jesus_of_Prague" title="Infant Jesus of Prague">Infant Jesus of Prague</a> statue, according to tradition, such a statue is said to have been in her possession and Teresa is reputed to have given it to a noblewoman travelling to <a href="/wiki/Prague" title="Prague">Prague</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWongn.d._34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWongn.d.-34">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-pragjesu.info_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pragjesu.info-35">&#91;web 9&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-devotionsandprayers_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-devotionsandprayers-36">&#91;web 10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-saints.sqpn.com_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-saints.sqpn.com-37">&#91;web 11&#93;</a></sup> The age of the statue dates to approximately the same time as Teresa. It has been thought that Teresa carried a portable statue of the <a href="/wiki/Child_Jesus" class="mw-redirect" title="Child Jesus">Child Jesus</a> wherever she went; the idea circulated by the early 1700s.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESantini1995_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESantini1995-38">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (October 2020)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Writings">Writings</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Writings">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Teresa_de_Jes%C3%BAs.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a0/Teresa_de_Jes%C3%BAs.jpg/170px-Teresa_de_Jes%C3%BAs.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="246" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a0/Teresa_de_Jes%C3%BAs.jpg/255px-Teresa_de_Jes%C3%BAs.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a0/Teresa_de_Jes%C3%BAs.jpg/340px-Teresa_de_Jes%C3%BAs.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="1159" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Teresa_de_Jes%C3%BAs.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>This is the one portrait of Teresa that is probably the most true to her appearance. It is a copy of an original 1576 painting of her when she was 61.</div></div></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Autobiography">Autobiography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Autobiography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The autobiography, <i>La Vida de la Santa Madre Teresa de Jesús</i> (<i>The Life of the Holy Mother Teresa of Jesus</i>) was written between composed at Avila between 1562 and 1565, but published posthumously.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Editions include:
</p>
<ul><li><i>The Life of St. Teresa of Jesus ... Written by Herself.</i> Translated from the Spanish by D. Lewis, 1870. London: Burns, Oates, &amp; Co</li>
<li>The <i>Autobiography</i>, written before 1567, under the direction of her confessor, Fr. Pedro Ibáñez, 1882</li>
<li><i>The Life Of Saint Teresa Of Avila By Herself</i>. J.M. Cohen, 1957. Penguin Classics</li>
<li><i>Life of St. Teresa of Jesus</i>. Translated by Benedict Zimmerman, 1997. Tan Books, <style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1133582631">.mw-parser-output cite.citation{font-style:inherit;word-wrap:break-word}.mw-parser-output .citation q{quotes:"\"""\"""'""'"}.mw-parser-output .citation:target{background-color:rgba(0,127,255,0.133)}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-free a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-free a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/65/Lock-green.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-limited a,.mw-parser-output .id-lock-registration a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-limited a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-registration a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/d6/Lock-gray-alt-2.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-subscription a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-subscription a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/a/aa/Lock-red-alt-2.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .cs1-ws-icon a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/4/4c/Wikisource-logo.svg")right 0.1em center/12px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .cs1-code{color:inherit;background:inherit;border:none;padding:inherit}.mw-parser-output .cs1-hidden-error{display:none;color:#d33}.mw-parser-output .cs1-visible-error{color:#d33}.mw-parser-output .cs1-maint{display:none;color:#3a3;margin-left:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .cs1-format{font-size:95%}.mw-parser-output .cs1-kern-left{padding-left:0.2em}.mw-parser-output .cs1-kern-right{padding-right:0.2em}.mw-parser-output .citation .mw-selflink{font-weight:inherit}</style><a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-89555-603-5" title="Special:BookSources/978-0-89555-603-5">978-0-89555-603-5</a></li>
<li><i>The Life of Teresa of Jesus: The Autobiography of Teresa of Avila</i>. Translated by E. Allison Peers, 1991. Doubleday, <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-385-01109-9" title="Special:BookSources/978-0-385-01109-9">978-0-385-01109-9</a></li>
