Indianapolis/Cambridge: <a href="/wiki/Hackett_Publishing_Company" title="Hackett Publishing Company">Hackett Publishing Company</a>, <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-87220-907-7" title="Special:BookSources/978-0-87220-907-7">978-0-87220-907-7</a></li>
<li><i>The Book of My Life</i>. Mirabai Starr, 2008. Boston, Massachusetts: Shambhala Publications, <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-1-59030-573-7" title="Special:BookSources/978-1-59030-573-7">978-1-59030-573-7</a></li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="The_Way_of_Perfection">The Way of Perfection</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: The Way of Perfection">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p><i>The Way of Perfection</i> (<i><a href="/wiki/Spanish_language" title="Spanish language">Spanish</a>: <i lang="es">Camino de Perfección</i></i>) was published in 1566. Teresa called this a "living book" and in it set out to teach her nuns how to progress through prayer and <a href="/wiki/Christian_meditation" title="Christian meditation">Christian meditation</a>. She discusses the rationale for being a Carmelite, and the rest deals with the purpose of and approaches to spiritual life. The title was inspired by the devotional book <i><a href="/wiki/The_Imitation_of_Christ" title="The Imitation of Christ">The Imitation of Christ</a></i> (1418) which had become a favourite expression of Teresa much before she wrote this work, as it appeared at several places in her autobiography, <i>The Life of Teresa of Jesus</i>. Like her other books, <i>The Way of Perfection</i> was written on the advice of her counsellors to describe her experiences in prayer during the period when the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Reformation" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholic Reformation">Reformation</a> was spreading through Europe. Herein she describes ways of attaining spiritual perfection through <a href="/wiki/Mental_prayer" title="Mental prayer">prayer</a> and its four stages, as in <a href="/wiki/Christian_meditation" title="Christian meditation">meditation</a>, <a href="/wiki/Prayer_of_Quiet" class="mw-redirect" title="Prayer of Quiet">quiet</a>, repose of soul and finally perfect <a href="/wiki/Union_with_God" class="mw-redirect" title="Union with God">union with God</a>, which she equates with rapture.
</p>
<dl><dt>Editions</dt>
<dd></dd></dl>
<ul><li><i>The Way of Perfection</i>. Translated and Edited by E. Allison Peers, Doubleday, 1991. <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-385-06539-9" title="Special:BookSources/978-0-385-06539-9">978-0-385-06539-9</a></li>
<li><i>The Way of Perfection</i>, <a href="/wiki/TAN_Books" title="TAN Books">TAN Books</a>, 1997. <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-89555-602-8" title="Special:BookSources/978-0-89555-602-8">978-0-89555-602-8</a></li>
<li><i>Way of Perfection</i>, London, 2012. limovia.net <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-1-78336-025-3" title="Special:BookSources/978-1-78336-025-3">978-1-78336-025-3</a></li>
<li><i>El <a href="/wiki/Camino_de_Perfecci%C3%B3n" class="mw-redirect" title="Camino de Perfección">Camino de Perfección</a></i> (<i>The Way of Perfection</i>), written also before 1567, at the direction of her confessor.</li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Interior_Castle">Interior Castle</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Interior Castle">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The <i>Interior Castle</i>, or <i>The Mansions</i>, (<i><a href="/wiki/Spanish_language" title="Spanish language">Spanish</a>: <i lang="es">El Castillo Interior</i></i> or <i>Las Moradas</i>) was written in 1577, and published in 1588.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> It contained the basis for what she felt should be the ideal journey of <a href="/wiki/Faith_in_Christianity" title="Faith in Christianity">faith</a>, comparing the contemplative <a href="/wiki/Soul" title="Soul">soul</a> to a <a href="/wiki/Castle" title="Castle">castle</a> with seven successive interior courts, or chambers, analogous to the seven mansions. The work was inspired by her vision of the soul as a diamond in the shape of a castle containing seven mansions, which she interpreted as the journey of faith through seven stages, ending with union with God.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDetweilerJasper200048_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDetweilerJasper200048-42">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Fray Diego, one of Teresa's former <a href="/wiki/Confessor" title="Confessor">confessors</a> wrote that God revealed to Teresa:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>...a most beautiful crystal globe, made in the shape of a castle, and containing seven mansions, in the seventh and innermost of which was the King of Glory, in the greatest splendour, illumining and beautifying them all. The nearer one got to the centre, the stronger was the light; outside the palace limits everything was foul, dark and infested with toads, vipers and other venomous creatures."<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p><a href="/wiki/Christia_Mercer" title="Christia Mercer">Christia Mercer</a>, Columbia University philosophy professor, claims that the seventeenth-century Frenchman <a href="/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_Descartes" title="René Descartes">René Descartes</a> lifted some of his most influential ideas from Teresa of Ávila, who, fifty years before Descartes, wrote popular books about the role of philosophical reflection in intellectual growth.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMercer20172539–2555_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMercer20172539–2555-44">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> She describes a number of striking similarities between Descartes's seminal work <i><a href="/wiki/Meditations_on_First_Philosophy" title="Meditations on First Philosophy">Meditations on First Philosophy</a></i> and Teresa's <i>Interior Castle</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-CM2017_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CM2017-45">&#91;web 12&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Translations">Translations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Translations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<ul><li>The first English translation was published in 1675.</li>
<li>Fr. John Dalton (1852). John Dalton’s translation of <i>The Interior Castle</i> contains an interesting preface and translations of other letters by St. Teresa.</li>
<li>Benedictines of Stanbrook, edited by Fr. Zimmerman (1921). The translation of <i>The Interior Castle</i> by the Benedictines of Stanbrook also has an excellent introduction and includes many cross-references to other works by St. Teresa.</li>
<li>E. Allison Peers (1946). E. Allison Peers’ translation of <i>The Interior Castle</i> is another popular public domain version translated by a professor and scholar of Hispanic studies.</li>
<li>Fr. Kieran Kavanaugh (1979). This translation also stays true to the text and contains many useful cross-references. An <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.amazon.com/Interior-Castle-Revised-Translated-Kavanaugh/dp/1939272807/ref=sr_1_2_sspa?crid=3RWD78VXQBYCC&amp;keywords=the+interior+castle&amp;qid=1643942725&amp;s=books&amp;sprefix=the+interior+castle%2Cstripbooks%2C191&amp;sr=1-2-spons&amp;psc=1&amp;spLa=ZW5jcnlwdGVkUXV">updated study edition</a> contains comprehensive notes, reflection questions and a glossary.</li>
<li><i>The Interior Castle – The Mansions</i>, <a href="/wiki/TAN_Books" title="TAN Books">TAN Books</a>, 1997. <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-89555-604-2" title="Special:BookSources/978-0-89555-604-2">978-0-89555-604-2</a></li>
<li>Mirabai Starr (2004). Described as "free of religious dogma, this modern translation renders St. Teresa's work a beautiful and practical set of teachings for seekers of all faiths in need of spiritual guidance." <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.amazon.com/Interior-Castle-Teresa-Avila/dp/1594480052/ref=sr_1_1?crid=3RWD78VXQBYCC&amp;keywords=the+interior+castle&amp;qid=1643942816&amp;s=books&amp;sprefix=the+interior+castle%2Cstripbooks%2C191&amp;sr=1-1">Starr’s interpretive version of <i>The Interior Castle</i></a> eliminates Teresa’s use of words such as “sin”, which results in a translation which is more paraphrased than accurate translation.</li>
<li><i>The Interior Castle - Modern update of the spiritual guide by Teresa of Avila</i>. by M.B. Anderson, Root Classics (publisher), 2022. <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-1-956314-01-4" title="Special:BookSources/978-1-956314-01-4">978-1-956314-01-4</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;e&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="In_popular_culture">In popular culture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: In popular culture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>St. Teresa's mystical experiences have inspired several authors in modern times, but not necessarily from Teresa's Christian theological perspective.
</p>
<ul><li class="mw-empty-elt"></li></ul>
<p>The 2006 book <i><a href="/wiki/Eat,_Pray,_Love" title="Eat, Pray, Love">Eat, Pray, Love</a></i> by <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_Gilbert" title="Elizabeth Gilbert">Elizabeth Gilbert</a> recognizes St. Teresa as "that most mystical of Catholic figures" and alludes to St. Teresa's <i>Interior Castle</i> as the "mansions of her being" and her journey as one of "divine meditative bliss". Gilbert was raised a Protestant Christian, but her book describes her path to God through yoga.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li>The 2007 book by American spiritual author <a href="/wiki/Caroline_Myss" title="Caroline Myss">Caroline Myss</a> <i>Entering the Castle</i> was inspired by St. Teresa's <i>Interior Castle</i>, but still has a <a href="/wiki/New_Age" title="New Age">New Age</a> approach to mysticism.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>St. Teresa also inspired American author <a href="/wiki/R._A._Lafferty" title="R. A. Lafferty">R. A. Lafferty</a> in his novel <i><a href="/wiki/Fourth_Mansions" title="Fourth Mansions">Fourth Mansions</a></i> (1969), which was nominated for the <a href="/wiki/Nebula_Award" title="Nebula Award">Nebula Award</a> for <a href="/wiki/Nebula_Award_for_Best_Novel" title="Nebula Award for Best Novel">Best Novel</a> in 1970.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Brooke_Fraser" title="Brooke Fraser">Brooke Fraser</a>'s song "Orphans, Kingdoms" was inspired by St. Teresa's <i>Interior Castle</i>.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Jean_Stafford" title="Jean Stafford">Jean Stafford</a>'s short story 'The Interior Castle' relates the intense preoccupation of an accident victim with her own brain, which she sees variously as a jewel, a flower, a light in a glass and a set of envelopes within envelopes.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Jeffrey_Eugenides" title="Jeffrey Eugenides">Jeffrey Eugenides</a>' 2011 novel <i><a href="/wiki/The_Marriage_Plot" title="The Marriage Plot">The Marriage Plot</a></i> refers to St. Teresa's <i>Interior Castle</i> when recounting the religious experience of Mitchell Grammaticus, one of the main characters of the book.</li>
<li>Teen Daze's <sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> 2012 release "The Inner Mansions" refers to St. Teresa's <i>Interior Castle</i> in the album's title as well as in the first track. <i>"...have mercy on yourselves! If you realize your pitiable condition, how can you refrain from trying to remove the darkness from the crystal of your souls? Remember, if death should take you now, you would never again enjoy the light of this Sun."</i><sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> This line appears dubbed over the musical introduction to "New Life."<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>In Mark Williamson's "ONE: a memoir" (2018), the metaphor of the Interior Castle is used to describe an inner world of introspective reflection on past events, a set of "memory loci" based on the ancient system of recall for rhetorical purposes.</li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Other">Other</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Other">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li><i>Relaciones</i> (<i>Relationships</i>), an extension of the autobiography giving her inner and outer experiences in epistolary form.</li>
<li>Her rare poems (<i>"Todas las poesías"</i>, <a href="/wiki/Munster" title="Munster">Munster</a>, 1854) are distinguished for tenderness of feeling and rhythm of thought.</li>
<li><i>The Complete Poetry of St. Teresa of Avila</i>. A Bilingual Edition – Edición y traducción de Eric W. Vogt. <a href="/wiki/New_Orleans_University" title="New Orleans University">New Orleans University</a> Press of the South, 1996. Second edition, 2015. xl, 116 p. <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-1-937030-52-0" title="Special:BookSources/978-1-937030-52-0">978-1-937030-52-0</a></li>
<li>"Meditations on Song of Songs", 1567, written nominally for her daughters at the convent of Our Lady of Mount Carmel.</li>
<li><i>Conceptos del Amor</i> ("Concepts of Love") and</li>
<li><i>Exclamaciones</i>.</li>
<li><i>Las Cartas</i> (Saragossa, 1671), or her correspondence, of which there are 342 extant letters and 87 fragments of others. The first edition of Teresa's letters was published in 1658 with the comment of <a href="/wiki/Juan_de_Palafox_y_Mendoza" title="Juan de Palafox y Mendoza">Juan de Palafox y Mendoza</a>, Roman Catholic bishop of Osma and an opponent to the Company of Jesus.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGarriga_Espino201535–53_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGarriga_Espino201535–53-53">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><i>The Complete Works of St Teresa of Jesus</i>, in five volumes, translated and edited by <a href="/wiki/Edgar_Allison_Peers" title="Edgar Allison Peers">E. Allison Peers</a>, including 2 volumes of correspondence. London: <a href="/wiki/Sheed_and_Ward" title="Sheed and Ward">Sheed and Ward</a>, 1982.</li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Mysticism">Mysticism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Mysticism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1062260506">.mw-parser-output .quotebox{background-color:#F9F9F9;border:1px solid #aaa;box-sizing:border-box;padding:10px;font-size:88%;max-width:100%}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatleft{margin:.5em 1.4em .8em 0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatright{margin:.5em 0 .8em 1.4em}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.centered{overflow:hidden;position:relative;margin:.5em auto .8em auto}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatleft span,.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatright span{font-style:inherit}.mw-parser-output .quotebox>blockquote{margin:0;padding:0;border-left:0;font-family:inherit;font-size:inherit}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-title{background-color:#F9F9F9;text-align:center;font-size:110%;font-weight:bold}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote>:first-child{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote:last-child>:last-child{margin-bottom:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote.quoted:before{font-family:"Times New Roman",serif;font-weight:bold;font-size:large;color:gray;content:" “ ";vertical-align:-45%;line-height:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote.quoted:after{font-family:"Times New Roman",serif;font-weight:bold;font-size:large;color:gray;content:" ” ";line-height:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .left-aligned{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .right-aligned{text-align:right}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .center-aligned{text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .quote-title,.mw-parser-output .quotebox .quotebox-quote{display:block}.mw-parser-output .quotebox cite{display:block;font-style:normal}@media screen and (max-width:640px){.mw-parser-output .quotebox{width:100%!important;margin:0 0 .8em!important;float:none!important}}</style><div class="quotebox pullquote floatright" style="width:20em; ; background-color: #FFE0BB;">
<blockquote class="quotebox-quote left-aligned" style="">
<p>The prayer <i>Nada te turbe</i> (Let nothing disturb you) is attributed to Teresa, having been found within her breviary:<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;web 13&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><div class="poem">
<p>Let nothing disturb you.<br />
Let nothing make you afraid.<br />
All things are passing.<br />
God alone never changes.<br />
Patience gains all things.<br />
If you have God you will want for nothing.<br />
God alone suffices.<a href="#CITEREFKirvan1996">Kirvan 1996</a>
</p>
</div><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8201;<cite>Teresa of Ávila</cite></div></blockquote>
</blockquote>
</div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Christian_contemplation" class="mw-redirect" title="Christian contemplation">Christian contemplation</a></div>
<p>The ultimate preoccupation of Teresa's mystical thought, as consistently reflected in her writings, is the ascent of the <a href="/wiki/Soul_(spirit)" class="mw-redirect" title="Soul (spirit)">soul</a> to God. Aumann notes that "the grades of prayer described in <i>The Life</i> do not correspond to the division of prayer commonly given in the manuals of spiritual life," due to the fact that "St. Teresa did not write a systematic theology of prayer."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAumann1982362_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAumann1982362-55">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> According to Zimmerman, "In all her writings on this subject she deals with her personal experiences [...] there is no vestige in her writings of any influence of the <a href="/wiki/Pseudo-Dionysius_the_Areopagite" title="Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite">Areopagite</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Church_Fathers" title="Church Fathers">Patristic</a>, or the <a href="/wiki/Scholasticism" title="Scholasticism">Scholastic</a> Mystical schools, as represented among others, by the <a href="/wiki/Friends_of_God" title="Friends of God">German Dominican Mystics</a>. She is intensely personal, her system going exactly as far as her experiences, but not a step further."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZimmerman2012_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZimmerman2012-56">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Teresa describes in the <i>Interior Castle</i> that the treasure of heaven lies buried within our hearts, and that there is an interior part of the heart which is the centre of the soul.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-20">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Four_stages_as_described_in_the_autobiography">Four stages as described in the <i>autobiography</i></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Four stages as described in the autobiography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In her autobiography she describes four stages,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHerzogSchaffHauck1908413_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHerzogSchaffHauck1908413-57">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> in which she uses the image of watering one's garden as a metaphor for mystical prayer:<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;f&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li>The first, <i>Devotion of the Heart</i>, consists of <a href="/wiki/Mental_prayer" title="Mental prayer">mental prayer</a> and meditation. It means the withdrawal of the soul from without, <a href="/wiki/Penitence" class="mw-redirect" title="Penitence">penitence</a> and especially the devout <a href="/wiki/Meditations_on_the_Life_of_Christ" title="Meditations on the Life of Christ">meditation</a> on the passion of Christ (<i>Autobiography</i> 11.20).</li>
<li>The second, <i>Devotion of Peace</i>, is where human will is surrendered to God. This occurs by virtue of an uplifted awareness granted by God, while other faculties, such as memory, reason, and imagination, are not yet safe from worldly distraction. Although a partial distraction can happen, due to outer activity such as repetition of prayers or writing down spiritual things, the prevailing state is one of quietude (<i>Autobiography</i> 14.1).</li>
<li>The third, <i>Devotion of Union</i>, concerns the absorption-in-God. It is not only a heightened, but essentially, an <a href="/wiki/Religious_ecstasy" title="Religious ecstasy">ecstatic</a> state. At this level, reason is also surrendered to God, and only the memory and imagination are left to ramble. This state is characterized by a blissful peace, a sweet slumber of at least the <i>higher soul faculties</i>, that is a consciousness of being enraptured by the love of God.</li>
<li>The fourth, <i>Devotion of Ecstasy</i>, is where the consciousness of being in the body disappears. Sensory faculties cease to operate. Memory and imagination also become absorbed in God, as though intoxicated. Body and spirit dwell in the throes of exquisite pain, alternating between a fearful fiery glow, in complete unconscious helplessness, and periods of apparent strangulation. Sometimes such ecstatic transports literally cause the body to be lifted into space.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEClissold198263–64_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClissold198263–64-59">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> This state may last as long as half an hour and tends to be followed by relaxation of a few hours of swoon-like weakness, attended by the absence of all faculties while in union with God. The subject awakens from this trance state in tears. It may be regarded as the culmination of mystical experience. Indeed, Teresa was said to have been observed <a href="/wiki/Levitation_(paranormal)" title="Levitation (paranormal)">levitating</a> during <a href="/wiki/Mass_(liturgy)" title="Mass (liturgy)">Mass</a> on more than one occasion.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEClissold198263–64_59-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClissold198263–64-59">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="The_seven_mansions_of_the_Interior_castle">The seven mansions of the Interior castle</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: The seven mansions of the Interior castle">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The <i>Interior Castle</i> is divided into seven mansions (also called dwelling places), each level describing a step to get closer to God. In her work, Teresa already assumed entrance into the first mansions by <a href="/wiki/Prayer" title="Prayer">prayer</a> and <a href="/wiki/Christian_meditation" title="Christian meditation">meditation</a>.
</p><p>The purgative stage, involving active prayer and <a href="/wiki/Asceticism" title="Asceticism">asceticism</a>:<sup id="cite_ref-CS_seven_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CS_seven-60">&#91;web 14&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li>The first mansion begin with a <a href="/wiki/Soul" title="Soul">soul</a>'s state of <a href="/wiki/Divine_grace" title="Divine grace">grace</a>, but the souls are surrounded by <a href="/wiki/Sin" title="Sin">sin</a> and only starting to seek God's grace through <a href="/wiki/Humility#Christianity" title="Humility">humility</a> in order to achieve perfection.</li>
<li>The second mansions are also called the <i>Mansion of the Practice of Prayer</i> because the soul seeks to advance through the castle by daily thoughts of God, humble recognition of God's work in the soul and ultimately daily prayer.</li>
<li>The third mansions are the Mansions of Exemplary Life characterized through <a href="/wiki/Divine_grace" title="Divine grace">divine grace</a> and a <a href="/wiki/Charity_(virtue)" class="mw-redirect" title="Charity (virtue)">love</a> for God that is so great that the soul has an aversion to both mortal and venial sin and a desire to do works of charitable service to man for the ultimate glory of God. The prayer of acquired recollection belongs to the third mansion.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAumann1982366_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAumann1982366-61">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<p>The illuminative stage, the beginning of mystical or contemplative or supernatural prayer:<sup id="cite_ref-CS_seven_60-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CS_seven-60">&#91;web 14&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li>The fourth mansions are a departure from the soul actively acquiring what it gains as God increases his role. In this mansion, the soul begins to experience two types of supernatural prayer, namely the <i>Prayer of Supernatural (or passive)&#160;Recollection</i> and <i>The Prayer of Quiet</i>;<sup id="cite_ref-CS_seven_60-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CS_seven-60">&#91;web 14&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The fifth mansion is <i>The Prayer of Union</i>, in which the soul prepares itself to receive gifts from God;</li></ul>
<p>Unitive stage:<sup id="cite_ref-CS_seven_60-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CS_seven-60">&#91;web 14&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li>The sixth mansion is the betrothal (engagement) of the soul with God can be compared to lovers. The soul spends increasing amounts of time torn between favors from God and from outside afflictions.</li>
<li>The seventh mansion is the spiritual marriage with God, in which the soul achieves clarity in prayer</li></ul>
<p>Some scholars have compared the seven mansions to the seven chakras in Hindu culture.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Nine_grades_of_prayer">Nine grades of prayer</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Nine grades of prayer">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Overview">Overview</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Overview">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>The first four grades of Teresa's classifications of prayer belong to the ascetical stage of spiritual life. These are vocal prayer, meditational or mental prayer, affective prayer, and acquired or natural recollection.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoulain1908_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoulain1908-63">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAumann1980_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAumann1980-64">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-CC_Nine_Grades_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CC_Nine_Grades-65">&#91;web 15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to Augustin Poulain and Robert Thouless, Teresa described four degrees or stages of mystical union, namely the <a href="/wiki/Prayer_of_quiet" class="mw-redirect" title="Prayer of quiet">prayer of quiet</a>, full or semi-ecstatic union, ecstatic union or ecstasy, and transforming or deifying union, or spiritual marriage (properly) of the soul with God.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoulain1908_63-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoulain1908-63">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThouless1971125_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThouless1971125-66">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> TWhile Augustin Poulain and Robert Thouless don't mention the <i>Prayer of Supernatural (or passive)&#160;Recollection</i> as a separate stage,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoulain1908_63-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoulain1908-63">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThouless1971125_66-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThouless1971125-66">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Aumann discerns infused contemplation as a separate stage in the fourth mansion of the <i>Interior Castle</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAumann1980_64-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAumann1980-64">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-CC_Nine_Grades_65-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CC_Nine_Grades-65">&#91;web 15&#93;</a></sup> Together, these "five grades are infused prayer and belong to the mystical phase of spiritual life."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAumann1980_64-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAumann1980-64">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-CC_Nine_Grades_65-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CC_Nine_Grades-65">&#91;web 15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Thomas Merton disagrees on a fine-cut distinction between acquired contemplation and the prayer of quiet, noticing the Carmelite tendency of systematization, whereas Teresa herself was just describing her personal experiences.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMerton2008245-246_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMerton2008245-246-67">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Commenting on Teresa's writings and the scholarly discussions on the precise stages, <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Merton" title="Thomas Merton">Thomas Merton</a> comments: "with all these divisions and distinctions, comings and goings and varieties of terms, one tends to become impatient with the saint."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMerton2008241-242_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMerton2008241-242-68">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Aumann synthesizes Teresa's writings into nine grades of prayer:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAumann1980_64-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAumann1980-64">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAumann1982_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAumann1982-69">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-CC_Nine_Grades_65-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CC_Nine_Grades-65">&#91;web 15&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<table border="1" cellpadding="5" cellspacing="0">

<tbody><tr>

<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Ordinary_prayer_or_ascetical_stage">Ordinary prayer or ascetical stage</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Ordinary prayer or ascetical stage">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Mental_or_meditational_prayer">Mental or meditational prayer</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Mental or meditational prayer">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h5>
<p>Mental prayer is a form of <a href="/wiki/Prayer" title="Prayer">prayer</a> "performed without aid of any particular formula."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESimler2009_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESimler2009-70">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> It is distinguished from vocal prayers, "prayers performed by means of a given formula,"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESimler2009_70-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESimler2009-70">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Prayer is mental when the thoughts and affections of the soul are not expressed in a previously determined formula.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;web 16&#93;</a></sup> According to Teresa of Ávila, mental prayer is meditational prayer, in which the person is like a gardener, who, with much labour, draws the water up from the depths of the well to water the plants and flowers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWynne1911_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWynne1911-72">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELehodey191213_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELehodey191213-73">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> According to Teresa of Avila, mental prayer can proceed by using vocal prayers in order to improve dialogue with God.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETeresa_of_Ávila2007141_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETeresa_of_Ávila2007141-74">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> According to Lehodey, mental prayer can be divided into meditation, more active in reflections, and contemplation, more quiet and gazeful.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELehodey19125_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELehodey19125-75">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Natural_or_acquired_contemplation_-_prayer_of_simplicity">Natural or acquired contemplation - prayer of simplicity</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Natural or acquired contemplation - prayer of simplicity">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h5>
<p>For Teresa of Avila, in natural or acquired contemplation, also called the prayer of simplicity<sup id="cite_ref-prayer_of_simplicity_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-prayer_of_simplicity-77">&#91;g&#93;</a></sup> there is one dominant thought or sentiment which recurs constantly and easily (although with little or no development) amid many other thoughts, beneficial or otherwise. The prayer of simplicity often has a tendency to simplify itself even in respect to its object, leading one to think chiefly of God and of his presence, but in a confused manner.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoulain1908_63-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoulain1908-63">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the words of Saint <a href="/wiki/Alphonsus_Maria_de_Liguori" class="mw-redirect" title="Alphonsus Maria de Liguori">Alphonsus Maria de Liguori</a>, acquired contemplation "consists in seeing at a simple glance the truths which could previously be discovered only through prolonged discourse": reasoning is largely replaced by intuition and affections and resolutions, though not absent, are only slightly varied and expressed in a few words. Similarly, Saint <a href="/wiki/Ignatius_of_Loyola" title="Ignatius of Loyola">Ignatius of Loyola</a>, in his 30-day retreat or <a href="/wiki/Spiritual_Exercises_of_Ignatius_of_Loyola" class="mw-redirect" title="Spiritual Exercises of Ignatius of Loyola">Spiritual Exercises</a> beginning in the "second week" with its focus on the life of Jesus, describes less reflection and more simple contemplation on the events of Jesus' life. These contemplations consist mainly in a simple gaze and include an "application of the senses" to the events,<sup id="cite_ref-spirex_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-spirex-78">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 121">&#58;&#8202;121&#8202;</span></sup> to further one's empathy for Jesus' values, "to love him more and to follow him more closely."<sup id="cite_ref-spirex_78-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-spirex-78">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 104">&#58;&#8202;104&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>Definitions similar to that of Saint Alphonsus Maria de Liguori are given by Adolphe Tanquerey ("a simple gaze on God and divine things proceeding from love and tending thereto") and Saint <a href="/wiki/Francis_de_Sales" title="Francis de Sales">Francis de Sales</a> ("a loving, simple and permanent attentiveness of the mind to divine things").<sup id="cite_ref-Inner_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Inner-79">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Natural or acquired contemplation has been compared to the attitude of a mother watching over the cradle of her child: she thinks lovingly of the child without reflection and amid interruptions. The <i><a href="/wiki/Catechism_of_the_Catholic_Church" title="Catechism of the Catholic Church">Catechism of the Catholic Church</a></i> states: 
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>What is contemplative prayer? St. Teresa answers: 'Contemplative [sic]<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;h&#93;</a></sup> prayer [oración mental] in my opinion is nothing else than a close sharing between friends; it means taking time frequently to be alone with him who we know loves us.' Contemplative prayer seeks him 'whom my soul loves'. It is Jesus, and in him, the Father. We seek him, because to desire him is always the beginning of love, and we seek him in that pure faith which causes us to be born of him and to live in him. In this inner prayer we can still meditate, but our attention is fixed on the Lord himself.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Infused_or_higher_contemplation_-_mystical_union">Infused or higher contemplation - mystical union</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=30" title="Edit section: Infused or higher contemplation - mystical union">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>According to Hardon, infused contemplation is "A supernatural gift by which a person's mind and will become totally centered on God. Under this influence the intellect receives special insights into things of the spirit, and the affections are extraordinarily animated with divine love. Infused contemplation assumes the free co-operation of the human will."<sup id="cite_ref-MCD_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MCD-85">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> According to Poulain, it is a form of <a href="/wiki/Henosis" title="Henosis">mystical union with God</a>, a union characterized by the fact that it is God, and God only, who manifests Himself.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoulain1908_63-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoulain1908-63">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> According to Poulain, mystical grage may also manifestat as visions of the humanity of Christ or an angel or revelations of a future event, and include miraculous bodily phenomena sometimes observed in ecstatics.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoulain1908_63-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoulain1908-63">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In Teresa's mysticism, infused contemplation is described as a "divinely originated, general, non-conceptual, loving awareness of God."<sup id="cite_ref-Dubay_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dubay-86">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> According to Dubay:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>It is a wordless awareness and love that we of ourselves cannot initiate or prolong. The beginnings of this contemplation are brief and frequently interrupted by distractions. The reality is so unimposing that one who lacks instruction can fail to appreciate what exactly is taking place. Initial infused prayer is so ordinary and unspectacular in the early stages that many fail to recognize it for what it is. Yet with generous people, that is, with those who try to live the whole Gospel wholeheartedly and who engage in an earnest prayer life, it is common.<sup id="cite_ref-Dubay_86-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dubay-86">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>According to <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Dubay" title="Thomas Dubay">Thomas Dubay</a>, infused contemplation is the normal, ordinary development of <a href="/wiki/Christian_meditation" title="Christian meditation">discursive prayer</a> (mental prayer, meditative prayer), which it gradually replaces.<sup id="cite_ref-Dubay_86-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dubay-86">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> Dubay considers infused contemplation as common only among "those who try to live the whole Gospel wholeheartedly and who engage in an earnest prayer life". Other writers view contemplative prayer in its infused supernatural form as far from common. <a href="/wiki/John_Baptist_Scaramelli" class="mw-redirect" title="John Baptist Scaramelli">John Baptist Scaramelli</a>, reacting in the 17th century against <a href="/wiki/Quietism_(Christian_philosophy)" class="mw-redirect" title="Quietism (Christian philosophy)">quietism</a>, taught that <a href="/wiki/Asceticism" title="Asceticism">asceticism</a> and <a href="/wiki/Christian_mysticism" title="Christian mysticism">mysticism</a> are two distinct paths to perfection, the former being the normal, ordinary end of the Christian life, and the latter something extraordinary and very rare.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> Jordan Aumann considered that this idea of the two paths was "an innovation in spiritual theology and a departure from the traditional Catholic teaching".<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> And <a href="/wiki/Jacques_Maritain" title="Jacques Maritain">Jacques Maritain</a> proposed that one should not say that every mystic necessarily enjoys habitual infused contemplation in the mystical state, since the gifts of the Holy Spirit are not limited to intellectual operations.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="The_Prayer_of_Quiet">The Prayer of Quiet</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=31" title="Edit section: The Prayer of Quiet">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h5>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Shikantaza" title="Shikantaza">Shikantaza</a> and <a href="/wiki/Samadhi" title="Samadhi">Samadhi</a></div>
<p>For Teresa of Avila, the Prayer of Quiet is a state in which the soul experiences an extraordinary peace and rest, accompanied by delight or pleasure in contemplating <a href="/wiki/God" title="God">God</a> as present.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETeresa_of_Ávila177_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETeresa_of_Ávila177-90">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETeresa_of_Ávila1921104_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETeresa_of_Ávila1921104-91">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;web 17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> According to Poulain, "Mystical union will be called spiritual quiet when the Divine action is still too weak to prevent distractions: in a word, when the imagination still retains a certain liberty."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoulain1908_63-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoulain1908-63">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> According to Poulain, in incomplete mystical union, or the prayer of quiet or supernatural recollection, the action of God is not strong enough to prevent distractions, and the imagination still retains a certain liberty.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoulain1908_63-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoulain1908-63">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Full_or_semi-ecstatic_union">Full or semi-ecstatic union</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=32" title="Edit section: Full or semi-ecstatic union">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h5>
<p>According to Poulain, "Mystical union will be called [...] full union when its strength is so great that the soul is fully occupied with the Divine object, whilst, on the other hand, the senses continue to act (under these conditions, by making a greater or less effort, one can cease from prayer."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoulain1908_63-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoulain1908-63">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Ecstatic_union">Ecstatic union</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=33" title="Edit section: Ecstatic union">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h5>
<p>According to Poulain, "Mystical union will be called [...] ecstasy when communications with the external world are severed or nearly so (in this event one can no longer make voluntary movement nor energy from the state at will)."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoulain1908_63-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoulain1908-63">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Transforming_union">Transforming union</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=34" title="Edit section: Transforming union">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h5>
<p>The transforming union differs from the other three specifically and not merely in intensity. According to Poulain, "It consists in the habitual consciousness of a mysterious grace which all shall possess in heaven: the anticipation of the Divine nature. The soul is conscious of the Divine assistance in its superior supernatural operations, those of the intellect and the will. Spiritual marriage differs from spiritual espousals inasmuch as the first of these states is permanent and the second only transitory."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPoulain1908_63-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoulain1908-63">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Quotes">Quotes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=35" title="Edit section: Quotes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Teresa, who reported <a href="/wiki/Visions_of_Jesus_and_Mary" title="Visions of Jesus and Mary">visions of Jesus and Mary</a>, was a strong believer in the efficacy of <a href="/wiki/Holy_water" title="Holy water">holy water</a>, claiming to have used it with success to repel evil spirits and temptations. She wrote: "I know from frequent experience that there is nothing which puts devils to flight better than holy water."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETeresa_of_AvilaStarr2008238–241_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETeresa_of_AvilaStarr2008238–241-94">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A modern prayer <i>Christ has no body but yours</i>, though widely attributed to Teresa,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowell2009151_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowell2009151-95">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-jwj_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-jwj-96">&#91;web 18&#93;</a></sup> is not found in her writings.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKnowles2009684_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKnowles2009684-97">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Portrayals">Portrayals</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=36" title="Edit section: Portrayals">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Portrayals of Teresa include the following:
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Fran%C3%A7ois_G%C3%A9rard_-_St_Theresa_(detail).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/13/Fran%C3%A7ois_G%C3%A9rard_-_St_Theresa_%28detail%29.jpg/170px-Fran%C3%A7ois_G%C3%A9rard_-_St_Theresa_%28detail%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="208" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/13/Fran%C3%A7ois_G%C3%A9rard_-_St_Theresa_%28detail%29.jpg/255px-Fran%C3%A7ois_G%C3%A9rard_-_St_Theresa_%28detail%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/13/Fran%C3%A7ois_G%C3%A9rard_-_St_Theresa_%28detail%29.jpg/340px-Fran%C3%A7ois_G%C3%A9rard_-_St_Theresa_%28detail%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="734" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Fran%C3%A7ois_G%C3%A9rard_-_St_Theresa_(detail).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Detail of <i>St. Theresa</i>, 1827, by French painter <a href="/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_G%C3%A9rard" title="François Gérard">François Gérard</a></div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Georges_Jules_Victor_Clairin_(1843-1919),_Sarah_Bernhardt_(1844-1923)_in_%27%27Sainte_Therese_d%27Avila%27%27.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/Georges_Jules_Victor_Clairin_%281843-1919%29%2C_Sarah_Bernhardt_%281844-1923%29_in_%27%27Sainte_Therese_d%27Avila%27%27.jpg/220px-Georges_Jules_Victor_Clairin_%281843-1919%29%2C_Sarah_Bernhardt_%281844-1923%29_in_%27%27Sainte_Therese_d%27Avila%27%27.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="144" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/Georges_Jules_Victor_Clairin_%281843-1919%29%2C_Sarah_Bernhardt_%281844-1923%29_in_%27%27Sainte_Therese_d%27Avila%27%27.jpg/330px-Georges_Jules_Victor_Clairin_%281843-1919%29%2C_Sarah_Bernhardt_%281844-1923%29_in_%27%27Sainte_Therese_d%27Avila%27%27.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/Georges_Jules_Victor_Clairin_%281843-1919%29%2C_Sarah_Bernhardt_%281844-1923%29_in_%27%27Sainte_Therese_d%27Avila%27%27.jpg/440px-Georges_Jules_Victor_Clairin_%281843-1919%29%2C_Sarah_Bernhardt_%281844-1923%29_in_%27%27Sainte_Therese_d%27Avila%27%27.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1096" data-file-height="717" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Georges_Jules_Victor_Clairin_(1843-1919),_Sarah_Bernhardt_(1844-1923)_in_%27%27Sainte_Therese_d%27Avila%27%27.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Portrait of <a href="/wiki/Sarah_Bernhardt" title="Sarah Bernhardt">Sarah Bernhardt</a> as Thérèse in <i>La Vierge d'Avila</i> by <a href="/wiki/Catulle_Mend%C3%A8s" title="Catulle Mendès">Catulle Mendès</a> (1906)</div></div></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Literature">Literature</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=37" title="Edit section: Literature">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Simone_de_Beauvoir" title="Simone de Beauvoir">Simone de Beauvoir</a> singles out Teresa as a woman who truly lived life for herself (and perhaps the only woman to do so) in her book <i><a href="/wiki/The_Second_Sex" title="The Second Sex">The Second Sex</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBeauvoir2011729_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeauvoir2011729-98">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>She is mentioned prominently in <a href="/wiki/Kathryn_Harrison" title="Kathryn Harrison">Kathryn Harrison</a>'s novel <i>Poison</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHarrison1995_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarrison1995-99">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> The main character, Francisca De Luarca, is fascinated by her life.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Don_DeLillo" title="Don DeLillo">Don DeLillo</a> in <i><a href="/wiki/End_Zone" title="End Zone">End Zone</a></i> depicted Teresa as a saint who eats from a human skull to remind herself of final things.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/R._A._Lafferty" title="R. A. Lafferty">R. A. Lafferty</a> was strongly inspired by <i><a href="/wiki/El_Castillo_Interior" class="mw-redirect" title="El Castillo Interior">El Castillo Interior</a></i> when he wrote his novel <i>Fourth Mansions</i>. Quotations from St. Teresa's work are frequently used as chapter headings.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELafferty1999_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELafferty1999-100">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Pierre_Klossowski" title="Pierre Klossowski">Pierre Klossowski</a> prominently features Saint Teresa of Ávila in his metaphysical novel <i><a href="/wiki/The_Baphomet" title="The Baphomet">The Baphomet</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKlossowski1998_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlossowski1998-101">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/George_Eliot" title="George Eliot">George Eliot</a> compared Dorothea Brooke to St. Teresa in <i><a href="/wiki/Middlemarch" title="Middlemarch">Middlemarch</a></i> (1871–1872) and wrote briefly about the life and works of St. Teresa in the "Prelude" to the novel.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaynard200977–80_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaynard200977–80-102">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Thomas_Hardy" title="Thomas Hardy">Thomas Hardy</a> took Saint Teresa as the inspiration for much of the characterisation of the heroine Tess (Teresa) Durbeyfield, in <i><a href="/wiki/Tess_of_the_d%27Urbervilles" title="Tess of the d&#39;Urbervilles">Tess of the d'Urbervilles</a></i> (1891), most notably the scene in which she lies in a field and senses her soul ecstatically above her.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMoore199025_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoore199025-103">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The contemporary poet <a href="/wiki/Jorie_Graham" title="Jorie Graham">Jorie Graham</a> features Saint Teresa in the poem <i>Breakdancing</i> in her volume <i>The End of Beauty</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGraham1999_104-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGraham1999-104">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/B%C3%A1rbara_Mujica_(writer)" title="Bárbara Mujica (writer)">Bárbara Mujica</a>'s novel <i><a href="/wiki/Sister_Teresa_(novel)" title="Sister Teresa (novel)">Sister Teresa</a></i>, while not strictly hagiographical, is based upon Teresa's life.<sup id="cite_ref-washingtonindependentreviewofbooks.com_105-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-washingtonindependentreviewofbooks.com-105">&#91;web 19&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Timothy_Findley" title="Timothy Findley">Timothy Findley</a>'s 1999 novel <i><a href="/wiki/Pilgrim_(Timothy_Findley_novel)" class="mw-redirect" title="Pilgrim (Timothy Findley novel)">Pilgrim</a></i> features Saint Teresa as a minor character.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFindley1999_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFindley1999-106">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Vita_Sackville-West" title="Vita Sackville-West">Vita Sackville-West</a> wrote a double biography contrasting the two Carmelite Theresas, <i>The Eagle and the Dove</i>, re-issued in 2018.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESackville-West2011_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESackville-West2011-107">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFZepeda2012" class="citation book cs1">Zepeda, Reginald (2012). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=fhhi_t0cSsgC&amp;pg=PT69"><i>From Spain to Texas: A Cepeda y Ahumada Family Journey</i></a>. <a href="/wiki/Xlibris" title="Xlibris">Xlibris</a>. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/9781479770083" title="Special:BookSources/9781479770083"><bdi>9781479770083</bdi></a>.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=From+Spain+to+Texas%3A+A+Cepeda+y+Ahumada+Family+Journey&amp;rft.pub=Xlibris&amp;rft.date=2012&amp;rft.isbn=9781479770083&amp;rft.aulast=Zepeda&amp;rft.aufirst=Reginald&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%3Fid%3Dfhhi_t0cSsgC%26pg%3DPT69&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ATeresa+of+%C3%81vila" class="Z3988"></span><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Self-published_sources" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability"><span title="This reference citation appears to be to a self-published source. (July 2018)">self-published source</span></a></i>&#93;</sup></li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Painting_and_sculpture">Painting and sculpture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=38" title="Edit section: Painting and sculpture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li><i><a href="/wiki/Saint_Teresa_of_%C3%81vila%27s_Vision_of_the_Holy_Spirit" title="Saint Teresa of Ávila&#39;s Vision of the Holy Spirit">Saint Teresa of Ávila's Vision of the Holy Spirit</a></i> is a 1612-1614 painting by <a href="/wiki/Peter_Paul_Rubens" title="Peter Paul Rubens">Peter Paul Rubens</a> and is exhibited in the <a href="/wiki/Museum_Boijmans_Van_Beuningen" title="Museum Boijmans Van Beuningen">Museum Boijmans Van Beuningen</a> in <a href="/wiki/Rotterdam" title="Rotterdam">Rotterdam</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> and Rubens' c. 1614 <a href="/wiki/Saint_Teresa_of_%C3%81vila%27s_Vision_of_the_Holy_Spirit_(Cambridge)" title="Saint Teresa of Ávila&#39;s Vision of the Holy Spirit (Cambridge)">painting of the same subject</a> is in the <a href="/wiki/Fitzwilliam_Museum" title="Fitzwilliam Museum">Fitzwilliam Museum</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cambridge" title="Cambridge">Cambridge</a>.</li>
<li>Another Rubens portrait of Teresa, from 1615, is now in the collection of the <a href="/wiki/Kunsthistorisches_Museum" title="Kunsthistorisches Museum">Kunsthistorisches Museum</a>, <a href="/wiki/Vienna" title="Vienna">Vienna</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-khm.at_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-khm.at-109">&#91;web 20&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Saint Teresa was the inspiration for one of <a href="/wiki/Bernini" class="mw-redirect" title="Bernini">Bernini</a>'s most famous sculptures, <i><a href="/wiki/Ecstasy_of_Saint_Teresa" title="Ecstasy of Saint Teresa">The Ecstasy of St. Teresa</a></i> (mid-17th century) in <a href="/wiki/Santa_Maria_della_Vittoria,_Rome" title="Santa Maria della Vittoria, Rome">Santa Maria della Vittoria, Rome</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJordanova201279–94_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJordanova201279–94-110">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-lumenlearning.com_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lumenlearning.com-111">&#91;web 21&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><i>St. Teresa</i> was painted in 1819–20 by <a href="/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Pascal_Simon,_Baron_G%C3%A9rard" class="mw-redirect" title="François Pascal Simon, Baron Gérard">François Gérard</a>, a French neoclassical painter.<sup id="cite_ref-fraangelicoinstitute.com_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fraangelicoinstitute.com-112">&#91;web 22&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><i>St Theresa of Avila</i> is a 1754-1755 painting by <a href="/wiki/Joseph-Marie_Vien" title="Joseph-Marie Vien">Joseph-Marie Vien</a> and is exhibited in the <a href="/wiki/New_Orleans_Museum_of_Art" title="New Orleans Museum of Art">New Orleans Museum of Art</a> in New Orleans, Louisiana.</li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Drama_and_film">Drama and film</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=39" title="Edit section: Drama and film">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Hugh_Ross_Williamson" title="Hugh Ross Williamson">Hugh Ross Williamson</a> wrote a play, <i>Teresa of Avila</i>, about her life, which premiered in London in 1961.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Performance_art" title="Performance art">Performance artist</a> <a href="/wiki/Linda_Montano" title="Linda Montano">Linda Montano</a> has cited Teresa of Ávila as one of the most important influences on her work and since her return to Catholicism in the 2000s has done performances of her life.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKnowlesHeartneyMonkMontano200218–34_113-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKnowlesHeartneyMonkMontano200218–34-113">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/Teresa_de_Jes%C3%BAs_(film)" class="mw-redirect" title="Teresa de Jesús (film)">Teresa de Jesús</a></i> (1984), directed by <a href="/wiki/Josefina_Molina" title="Josefina Molina">Josefina Molina</a> and starring <a href="/wiki/Concha_Velasco" title="Concha Velasco">Concha Velasco</a>, is a Spanish made-for-TV mini-series. In it Teresa is portrayed as the determined foundress of new carmelite houses while protecting the <a href="/wiki/Infant_Jesus_of_Prague" title="Infant Jesus of Prague">infant Jesus</a> statue on her many arduous journeys. The devotion to the Child Jesus spread quickly in <a href="/wiki/Spain" title="Spain">Spain</a>, possibly due to her mystical reputation, and then to other places, including <a href="/wiki/France" title="France">France</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-imdb_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-imdb-114">&#91;web 23&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Nigel_Wingrove" title="Nigel Wingrove">Nigel Wingrove</a>'s 1989 short film <i><a href="/wiki/Visions_of_Ecstasy" title="Visions of Ecstasy">Visions of Ecstasy</a></i> was based on Teresa of Ávila. The film features phantasied sexualised scenes of Teresa with the body of <a href="/wiki/Jesus" title="Jesus">Jesus</a> on the cross. It is the only work to be refused certification by the <a href="/wiki/British_Board_of_Film_Classification" title="British Board of Film Classification">British Board of Film Classification</a> (BBFC) on the grounds of <a href="/wiki/Blasphemy" title="Blasphemy">blasphemy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-BBC_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBC-115">&#91;web 24&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Dževad Karahasan. <i>The Delighted Angel</i> drama about Teresa of Ávila and Rabija al-Adavija, Vienna-Salzburg-Klagenfurt, ARBOS 1995.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Paz_Vega" title="Paz Vega">Paz Vega</a> stars as Teresa in <i><a href="/wiki/Teresa,_el_cuerpo_de_Cristo" class="mw-redirect" title="Teresa, el cuerpo de Cristo">Teresa, el cuerpo de Cristo</a></i>, a 2007 Spanish <a href="/wiki/Biographical_film" title="Biographical film">biopic</a> directed by <a href="/wiki/Ray_Loriga" title="Ray Loriga">Ray Loriga</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Marian_%C3%81lvarez" title="Marian Álvarez">Marian Álvarez</a> plays the saint in <i>Teresa</i>, a 2015 Spanish <a href="/wiki/Television_film" title="Television film">television film</a> directed by <a href="/wiki/Jorge_Dorado" title="Jorge Dorado">Jorge Dorado</a> and made for the 500th anniversary of her birth.<sup id="cite_ref-sansebastianfestival.com_117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-sansebastianfestival.com-117">&#91;web 25&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>St. Teresa also features prominently in the 2009 Ron Howard film, <i>Angels and Demons</i>, where the Bernini sculpture, The Ecstasy of St. Teresa, is an important clue in helping Robert Langdon (Tom Hanks) find an anti-matter bomb that is hidden in and set to destroy the Vatican.</li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Music">Music</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=40" title="Edit section: Music">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Marc-Antoine_Charpentier" title="Marc-Antoine Charpentier">Marc-Antoine Charpentier</a> composed two <a href="/wiki/Motet" title="Motet">motets</a> for the feast of Saint Teresa: <i>Flores, flores o Gallia</i> for two voices, two flutes and continuo (H.374), c. 1680 and the other, for three voices and continuo (H.342), in 1686–87.</li>
<li>She is a principal character of the opera <i><a href="/wiki/Four_Saints_in_Three_Acts" title="Four Saints in Three Acts">Four Saints in Three Acts</a></i> by the composer <a href="/wiki/Virgil_Thomson" title="Virgil Thomson">Virgil Thomson</a> with a libretto by <a href="/wiki/Gertrude_Stein" title="Gertrude Stein">Gertrude Stein</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPark200928–44_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPark200928–44-118">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETommasini1998_119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETommasini1998-119">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Saint Teresa is the subject of the song "Theresa's Sound-World" by <a href="/wiki/Sonic_Youth" title="Sonic Youth">Sonic Youth</a> off the 1992 album <i><a href="/wiki/Dirty_(Sonic_Youth_album)" title="Dirty (Sonic Youth album)">Dirty</a></i>, lyrics by Thurston Moore.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2015)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHontiveros2016_120-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHontiveros2016-120">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><i>Saint Teresa</i> is a track on <a href="/wiki/Joan_Osborne" title="Joan Osborne">Joan Osborne</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/Relish_(album)" title="Relish (album)">Relish</a></i> album, nominated for a <a href="/wiki/Grammy_Award" class="mw-redirect" title="Grammy Award">Grammy Award</a> in 1996.<sup id="cite_ref-austriancharts.at_121-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-austriancharts.at-121">&#91;web 26&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Teresa_of_%C3%81vila&amp;action=edit&amp;section=42" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-columns references-column-width reflist-lower-alpha reflist-columns-2">
<ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-julianGregorian-4"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-julianGregorian_4-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">At some hour of the night between 4 October and 15 October 1582, the night of the transition in Spain from the Julian to the <a href="/wiki/Gregorian_calendar" title="Gregorian calendar">Gregorian calendar</a>.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-cherub-18"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-cherub_18-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Teresa wrote that it must be a <a href="/wiki/Cherub" title="Cherub">cherub</a> (<i>Deben ser los que llaman cherubines</i>), but Fr. Domingo Báñez wrote in the margin that it seemed more like a seraph (<i>mas parece de los que se llaman seraphis</i>), an identification that most editors have followed. <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFSanta_Teresa_de_Ávila" class="citation book cs1">Santa Teresa de Ávila. "<i>Libro de su vida</i>". <i>Escritos de Santa Teresa</i>.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=bookitem&amp;rft.atitle=Libro+de+su+vida&amp;rft.btitle=Escritos+de+Santa+Teresa&amp;rft.au=Santa+Teresa+de+%C3%81vila&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ATeresa+of+%C3%81vila" class="Z3988"></span> </span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-19"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-19">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">For the creation of the work and an analysis of its transgression of religious decorum, see Franco Mormando's article, <i>Did Bernini's 'Ecstasy of St. Teresa' Cross a 17th-century Line of Decorum?</i><a rel="nofollow" class="external autonumber" href="https://www.academia.edu/44401140/Did_Berninis_Ecstasy_of_St_Teresa_Cross_a_17th_century_Line_of_Decorum">[1]</a>).</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-29"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-29">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFRowe2011">Rowe 2011</a>, p.&#160;47 refers to the Castilian Cortes as the "Castilian parliament"</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-46"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-46">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Learn more about <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.rootclassics.com/theinteriorcastle">what was modified in the modern update of <i>The Interior Castle</i></a>.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-58"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-58">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">See: <i>The Autobiography</i> Chs. 10–22</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-prayer_of_simplicity-77"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-prayer_of_simplicity_77-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Catholic Dictionary: <i>Prayer of simplicity</i>: "Meditation replaced by a purer, more intimate prayer consisting in a simple regard or loving thought on God, or on one of his attributes, or on some mystery of the Christian faith. Reasoning is put aside and the soul peacefully attends to the operations of the Spirit with sentiments of love."<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> </span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-83"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-83">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Mental prayer, "oración mental," is not contemplative prayer.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWallenfangWallenfang2021&quot;B._Mental_Prayer_or_the_Prayer_of_meditation&quot;_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWallenfangWallenfang2021&quot;B._Mental_Prayer_or_the_Prayer_of_meditation&quot;-80">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHollenback1996535_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHollenback1996535-81">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWyhe2008174_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyhe2008174-82">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
</ol></div>
